And I work with people on computer skills—kind of training stuff—and I actually turned down a workshop because it was in a high-rise. I kept thinking about the building more than the workshop, which is ridiculous because the workshop itself would’ve been fine.\n\nAnd, um… I also stopped going to see one of my older computer students because his building doesn’t really have another option besides the elevator. He’s a nice guy too, always asks about these old desktop shortcuts like it’s still 2004, and I feel bad about it. But yeah, that’s why I’m here. It’s starting to mess with what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience panic or intense fear when you think about using an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s like… before I even really decide anything, my body reacts. If I step toward the elevator, or even when I hear that clunk when the doors open, my chest tightens up and my hands get sweaty.\n\nIt’s kind of embarrassing because it happens so fast, like I haven’t even had time to talk myself out of it. I’ll be standing there thinking about totally normal stuff, like whether I remembered to send someone a training link or whatever, and then the elevator noise hits and it’s just this rush of panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar panic or fear in other situations, like being in small rooms or high places?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, small rooms don’t usually bother me in the same way, I don’t think. Like, I’ve been in tiny supply closets at work looking for projector cables, and it’s annoying because everything’s tangled, but I’m not panicking.\n\nHigh places… I guess I haven’t really paid that much attention. I don’t love looking over edges, but I feel like most people don’t. It’s not like the elevator thing where my body just kind of takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or panicked if you were in a small, enclosed space that is not an elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’ve been in small spaces before and I don’t think I had the same reaction, but I guess it depends what you mean by enclosed.\n\nLike, if it’s just a small office or a storage room, I mostly just think about how cramped it is or how someone should really label the cables better—sorry, that’s random. But I haven’t really noticed myself panicking in those places the way I do with elevators.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space like an MRI machine?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve never had an MRI, so I don’t really know how I’d react in there.\n\nI mean, I’ve seen them on TV and they look pretty tight, and loud too, right? But that’s hard for me to compare because it’s not like standing in front of elevator doors waiting for them to open. I guess I might feel uncomfortable, but I don’t know if it would be that same panic.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space like a car with the windows rolled up?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been in cars with the windows up plenty of times, especially when it’s raining or cold, and I don’t think I’m sitting there freaking out or anything.\n\nAlthough if the air gets stuffy, I get annoyed pretty fast—my brother used to do this thing where he’d lock the windows from the driver’s side, which was just… honestly more irritating than scary. But yeah, I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety. It doesn’t feel like the same kind of situation to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small room with no windows and only one door\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. A small room with no windows sounds kind of uncomfortable, sure, but I’m not sure I’d panic just because of that.\n\nLike, if there’s one door and I know it opens, I’d probably be thinking more about why there are no windows—like those training rooms at work where the lighting is terrible and everyone gets sleepy after lunch. But I haven’t really noticed that being a big anxiety thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a subway car during rush hour\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t take the subway much—where I live it’s more buses or people driving everywhere. Rush hour sounds miserable though, like people packed in and everyone pretending not to notice each other.\n\nI had a coworker once who commuted like that every day, and she’d come in with this giant coffee and say the worst part was someone always having a backpack in her face, which… yeah, that sounds annoying. But I don’t know if I’d be anxious exactly, or just uncomfortable and wanting to get off because it’s crowded.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a crowded subway car during rush hour and the doors were closed\n\nPatient: I mean… crowded with the doors closed sounds pretty unpleasant, yeah. But I don’t really ride the subway enough to know how I’d react for sure.\n\nI keep picturing like, movies where everyone’s packed in and the train stops between stations, but I don’t know if that’s realistic or just TV making everything dramatic. I’d probably be more focused on somebody stepping on my shoes or blocking the door when it opens. So… anxious? Maybe uncomfortable, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that being a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space like a submarine?\n\nPatient: A submarine? I don’t really know. I’ve never been in one, so it’s hard to picture beyond, like, movies where everything is metal and there’s always some dramatic alarm going off.\n\nI guess it sounds cramped and not exactly pleasant, but I can’t say I know I’d be anxious. I’d probably be wondering how people even sleep down there, honestly—like the little bunks and all that. So… maybe uncomfortable, but I’m not sure beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space like a closet with the door closed?\n\nPatient: A closet with the door closed… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve been in closets before, like looking for coats or boxes, not like standing in there for a long time.\n\nIf it’s just for a minute, I think I’d probably be more annoyed than anything, like wondering why I’m in there. My mom used to keep all the Christmas stuff in this hallway closet, and every year it was like a puzzle trying to get the bins out without everything falling over. So that’s kind of what I picture.\n\nI guess if the door got stuck, that would be different, but just closed? I’m not sure I’d call it anxious.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were trapped in a small room with no way to get out\n\nPatient: Trapped, like actually no way to get out? I mean… yeah, that sounds bad in general, but I don’t know if I’ve ever been in that exact kind of situation.\n\nI’m trying to think of something similar, like when you’re in one of those little meeting rooms at work and someone books it with no windows, but even then there’s a door, and you can just leave. We had one room where the handle was kind of weird and you had to jiggle it, and everyone would make jokes about getting stuck in there, but nobody actually did.\n\nSo… I guess I’d probably be upset if I was truly trapped, but I’m not sure what my reaction would be beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space with no specific association to elevators?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, if it’s not an elevator, I don’t immediately picture myself panicking or anything. A small enclosed space could be a lot of things, you know—like a storage room, or those little sound booths people use for recording, which actually I always thought looked kind of cool because of the foam on the walls.\n\nI guess it would depend on the space? If I knew I could leave, I’d probably be okay, maybe just a little uncomfortable if it was really tight. But I don’t think I’ve noticed a big anxiety thing with small spaces in general.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space that has no association with elevators or heights?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it’s not an elevator and not something high up, I don’t really know. I’m trying to picture what that would be, like maybe a supply closet or one of those little rooms where they keep the copier paper. At work we have this tiny storage room and everyone just kind of shoves old binders in there, and it always smells like cardboard and dry-erase markers.\n\nBut anxiety-wise, I haven’t really noticed that being a thing for me. If it was super cramped I might want to get out just because it’s uncomfortable, but I don’t think I’d automatically feel anxious just because it’s enclosed.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space with no way out and no association with elevators or heights, but you knew help was available if needed?\n\nPatient: Hmm… with no way out but help available? I don’t know, that’s kind of a weird middle ground. Like, if I knew someone could come get me, I’d probably be telling myself that, you know, “Okay, you’re not actually stuck forever.”\n\nBut I haven’t really been in that situation. The closest I can think of is, like, when maintenance at our building puts those signs on doors and you’re not sure if a hallway is closed or not, but that’s not the same thing. And honestly I usually just get annoyed because it means I have to walk around the long way.\n\nSo… maybe I’d be uncomfortable, but I’m not sure I’d call it anxious unless it actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: For a long period of time… I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve tested that, exactly. Like, if I was in a small room for hours, I’d probably get restless, but I get restless in long meetings too, especially the training ones where the slides are just bullet points and someone reads them out loud.\n\nIf it was just small and enclosed, I don’t know that I’d feel anxious. Maybe bored or irritated, depending on whether I had my phone or if it was hot in there. But I haven’t noticed that being a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space, like a room, with no way to leave, but it was not an elevator?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, if it’s a room and not an elevator, I don’t have that same immediate reaction just from picturing it. The “no way to leave” part sounds unpleasant, obviously, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety or just… I’d probably be annoyed and start thinking about who designed the door situation.\n\nIt kind of reminds me of when conference rooms at work get booked back-to-back and you’re stuck waiting for someone with the key, and then everyone’s standing around with coffee cups acting like it’s a crisis. But that’s not really the same as actually having no way out.\n\nSo, I guess I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed small rooms themselves being a big problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were locked in a small room with no visible exit?\n\nPatient: I mean… locked in with no visible exit sounds bad, but I’m not sure how I’d react unless it actually happened. Like, part of me would probably be thinking, “Okay, where’s the door they’re not showing me?” because rooms usually have some kind of door, unless it’s like one of those weird escape room setups.\n\nActually, I did one of those once with coworkers for team-building, and everyone got way too intense about the clues. I mostly just stood there looking at the posters on the wall.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess I might be uncomfortable if there was really no visible exit, but I don’t know that I can say I’d definitely be anxious.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space with no visible exit, such as a windowless room?\n\nPatient: A windowless room… I mean, that sounds kind of unpleasant, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d jump straight to anxious. I’ve been in windowless training rooms before, like those office rooms with the buzzing lights and a whiteboard that still has someone’s half-erased flowchart on it, and mostly I just get bored or start noticing the air conditioning.\n\nThe “no visible exit” part is harder to picture, because even windowless rooms usually have a door, you know? If there really wasn’t one, I’d probably be confused first, like wondering how I got in there. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed windowless rooms themselves being a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were in a small, enclosed space like a windowless room with no door?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. A windowless room with no door is kind of hard for me to picture, honestly—like, how did I get in there in the first place? It sounds more like a magic trick or one of those weird building-code nightmare things.\n\nI mean, I’d probably be confused or irritated, maybe start looking around for a hidden panel or vent or something. I remember this one training room at work that had no windows and the door blended into the wall, and people kept missing it, which was funny for about two minutes and then annoying because everyone kept interrupting.\n\nBut if you mean literally no door, no way out… yeah, I don’t know. I haven’t really been in that situation, so I can’t say for sure I’d feel anxious."
